View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to find the MEX (minimum excluded) of a string - the shortest string that doesn't appear as a contiguous substring
2. If multiple strings exist, we choose the lexicographically smallest one
3. The empty string doesn't count as valid MEX
4. We need to check substrings in order of length (1, 2, 3, ...) and lexicographic order

## Approach

1. Start checking from length 1 substrings
2. For each length, generate all possible substrings of that length in lexicographic order
3. Check if each substring exists in the input string
4. Return the first substring that doesn't exist

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Generate all possible strings of a given length
For length 1: "a", "b", "c", ... "z"
For length 2: "aa", "ab", "ac", ... "az", "ba", "bb", ... "zz"

### Step 2: Check if a substring exists in the input
We need to check if the substring appears as a contiguous substring in the given string.

### Step 3: Find the lexicographically smallest missing substring
We check in lexicographic order and return the first one that doesn't appear.

### Step 4: Implementation

```python
def find_mex(s):
    n = len(s)
    
    # Start with length 1
    length = 1
    
    # Generate all possible substrings of current length in lexicographic order
    # For length 1: 'a', 'b', 'c', ..., 'z'
    # For length 2: 'aa', 'ab', 'ac', ..., 'az', 'ba', 'bb', ..., 'zz'
    
    # We'll check each possible string in lexicographic order
    from itertools import product
    
    # Check all possible strings of current length
    alphabet = 'abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz'
    
    # Try increasing lengths until we find a missing substring
    while True:
        # Generate all possible strings of current length in lexicographic order
        for chars in product(alphabet, repeat=length):
            substring = ''.join(chars)
            
            # Check if this substring appears in the input string
            if substring not in s:
                return substring
        
        # If we haven't found a missing substring of current length,
        # try with next length
        length += 1
        
        # Optimization: we can break early if length becomes too large
        # But the constraints are small (n <= 1000), so this should be fine

# Read input and solve
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n = int(input())
    s = input().strip()
    print(find_mex(s))
```

```python
def find_mex(s):
```
```python
    # Check all length 1 strings in lexicographic order
    for c in 'abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz':
        if c not in s:
            return c
    
    # Check all length 2 strings in lexicographic order
    for c1 in 'abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz':
        for c2 in 'abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz':
            substr = c1 + c2
            if substr not in s:
                return substr
    
    # Check all length 3 strings in lexicographic order  
    for c1 in 'abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz':
        for c2 in 'abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz':
            for c3 in 'abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz':
                substr = c1 + c2 + c3
                if substr not in s:
                    return substr
    
    # Fallback - should never reach here given the constraints
    return "a"

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n = int(input())
    s = input().strip()
    print(find_mex(s))
```

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Sergey Semyonovich is a mayor of a county city N and he used to spend his days and nights in thoughts of further improvements of Nkers' lives. Unfortunately for him, anything and everything has been done already, and there are no more possible improvements he can think of during the day (he now prefers to sleep at night). However, his assistants have found a solution and they now draw an imaginary city on a paper sheet and suggest the mayor can propose its improvements.

Right now he has a map of some imaginary city with $n$ subway stations. Some stations are directly connected with tunnels in such a way that the whole map is a tree (assistants were short on time and enthusiasm). It means that there exists exactly one simple path between each pair of station. We call a path simple if it uses each tunnel no more than once.

One of Sergey Semyonovich's favorite quality objectives is the sum of all pairwise distances between every pair of stations. The distance between two stations is the minimum possible number of tunnels on a path between them.

Sergey Semyonovich decided to add new tunnels to the subway map. In particular, he connected any two stations $u$ and $v$ that were not connected with a direct tunnel but share a common neighbor, i.e. there exists such a station $w$ that the original map has a tunnel between $u$ and $w$ and a tunnel between $w$ and $v$. You are given a task to compute the sum of pairwise distances between all pairs of stations in the new map.


-----Input-----

The first line of the input contains a single integer $n$ ($2 \leq n \leq 200\,000$) — the number of subway stations in the imaginary city drawn by mayor's assistants. Each of the following $n - 1$ lines contains two integers $u_i$ and $v_i$ ($1 \leq u_i, v_i \leq n$, $u_i \ne v_i$), meaning the station with these indices are connected with a direct tunnel.

It is guaranteed that these $n$ stations and $n - 1$ tunnels form a tree.


-----Output-----

Print one integer that is equal to the sum of distances between all pairs of stations after Sergey Semyonovich draws new tunnels between all pairs of stations that share a common neighbor in the original map.


-----Examples-----
Input
4
1 2
1 3
1 4

Output
6

Input
4
1 2
2 3
3 4

Output
7

In the first sample, in the new map all pairs of stations share a direct connection, so the sum of distances is $6$.

In the second sample, the new map has a direct tunnel between all pairs of stations except for the pair $(1, 4)$. I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a tree with n nodes (subway stations)
2. We add new edges between any two nodes that share a common neighbor in the original tree
3. We need to find the sum of all pairwise distances in the resulting graph

## Key Insights

1. In the original tree, two nodes share a common neighbor if they are both connected to the same node
2. After adding edges between all pairs that share a common neighbor, we create a new graph
3. The key observation is that if two nodes u and v share a common neighbor w, then they get a direct edge in the new graph

## Analysis of Examples

**Example 1:** Star graph (node 1 connected to 2, 3, 4)
- Original: 1-2, 1-3, 1-4
- Pairs sharing common neighbors:
  - 2 and 3 share neighbor 1 → add edge 2-3
  - 2 and 4 share neighbor 1 → add edge 2-4  
  - 3 and 4 share neighbor 1 → add edge 3-4
- Result: Complete graph K₄
- Distance between any two nodes: 1
- Sum = 4 pairs × 1 = 6 ✓

**Example 2:** Path graph (1-2-3-4)
- Original: 1-2, 2-3, 3-4
- Pairs sharing common neighbors:
  - 1 and 3 share neighbor 2 → add edge 1-3
  - 2 and 4 share neighbor 3 → add edge 2-4
  - Other pairs don't share common neighbors (1 and 4 don't share any neighbor)
- Result: Edges: 1-2, 2-3, 3-4, 1-3, 2-4
- Distance between 1 and 4: path 1-2-4 or 1-3-4, both length 2
- All other pairs: distance 1
- Sum = (1-2, 1-3, 1-4) + (2-3, 2-4) + (3-4) = 1+1+2 + 1+1 + 1 = 7 ✓

## Algorithm

The new graph will have edges:
1. All original tree edges
2. All edges between nodes that share a common neighbor

After adding these edges, the resulting graph has a special structure:
- Each connected component (which is the whole graph) will have a diameter of at most 2
- Actually, looking at the examples, the resulting graph might not be complete

Wait, let me reconsider. In the new graph, any two nodes that share a common neighbor get a direct edge. This creates a graph where:
- The diameter is at most 2 (since if two nodes are not directly connected, they must share a common neighbor in the original tree)

Actually, let me think more systematically:

Two nodes u and v get a direct edge if there exists w such that (u,w) and (v,w) are edges in original tree.

This means that if u and v have a common neighbor w, they become adjacent.

In the resulting graph, the distance between any two nodes is either:
- 1 (if they are directly connected)
- 2 (if they don't have a direct edge but are connected through some path)

The key insight is that from any node, we can reach any other node in at most 2 steps. This is because if u and v don't have a direct edge, they might still be close.

Actually, let me implement a more direct approach:

1. Build the original tree
2. For each node w, all pairs of nodes connected to w get a direct edge in the new graph
3. Calculate the sum of distances in the new graph

But calculating distances in the new graph directly would be O(n²). We need a more efficient approach.

## Efficient Solution

The key observation is that in the new graph:
- The sum of distances can be calculated by considering the contribution of each edge to the total distance sum.

However, since we're adding edges between nodes that share a neighbor, we can think about it differently:

After adding all these edges, the resulting graph has a property that any two nodes are at most distance 2 apart. In fact, if two nodes don't have a direct edge, they are connected through a common neighbor.

Actually, let me reconsider the structure: we add edges between all pairs that share a common neighbor. This creates a graph where each "star" center contributes to connecting all its leaves.

The resulting graph will have the property that:
- For a node w of degree d, all pairs of its neighbors get connected in the new graph
- So in the new graph, all pairs of neighbors of w are directly connected

So the new graph consists of all original edges plus edges between neighbors of the same original node.

## Implementation Strategy

1. Build adjacency list for original tree
2. For each node, identify all its neighbors
3. For each group of neighbors of a common node, they form a clique in the new graph
4. Calculate the sum of distances in this new graph

But calculating pairwise distances in the new graph is still challenging.

Wait, let me think differently. In the new graph:
- If two nodes are directly connected (original or added), distance = 1
- Otherwise, distance = 2

So sum of distances = (number of pairs with distance 1) + 2 × (number of pairs with distance 2)

Pairs with distance 1: all edges in the new graph
Pairs with distance 2: all pairs that are not directly connected

Total pairs: C(n,2) = n(n-1)/2

So sum = (edges in new graph) + 2 × (pairs not in new graph)

Number of edges in new graph = (original edges) + (added edges)

Original edges = n-1

Added edges: for each node w, if it has degree d, it contributes C(d,2) edges (pairs of its neighbors)

So total edges in new graph = (n-1) + Σ(C(deg(w),2)) for all w

Total pairs = n(n-1)/2

Pairs with distance 2 = total pairs - pairs with distance 1
= n(n-1)/2 - [(n-1) + Σ(C(deg(w),2))]

Wait, this is wrong because some pairs might have distance 1 for other reasons.

Actually, let me reframe:

In the new graph:
- Distance between u and v is 1 if they are directly connected (original or added)
- Distance between u and v is 2 otherwise

So sum of distances = (number of pairs with distance 1) + 2 × (number of pairs with distance 2)

Number of pairs with distance 1 = (original edges) + (added edges)
= (n-1) + Σ(C(deg(w),2))

Number of pairs with distance 2 = C(n,2) - (number of pairs with distance 1)

So final sum = (n-1 + Σ(C(deg(w),2))) + 2 × (C(n,2) - (n-1 + Σ(C(deg(w),2))))

Simplify: (n-1 + S) + 2×(n(n-1)/2 - (n-1 + S)) where S = Σ(C(deg(w),2))
= (n-1 + S) + 2×(n(n-1)/2 - n + 1 - S)
= (n-1 + S) + (n(n-1) - 2n + 2 - 2S)
= n-1 + S + n² - n - 2n + 2 - 2S
= n² - 3n + 1 - S

Wait, let me recalculate carefully:

Total pairs = n(n-1)/2

Pairs with distance 1 = (n-1) + Σ(C(deg(w),2)) = (n-1) + Σ(degree(w)×(degree(w)-1)/2)

Pairs with distance 2 = n(n-1)/2 - [(n-1) + Σ(degree(w)×(degree(w)-1)/2)]

Sum of distances = 1×(pairs with distance 1) + 2×(pairs with distance 2)
= (n-1) + Σ(C(deg(w),2)) + 2×(n(n-1)/2 - (n-1) - Σ(C(deg(w),2)))
= (n-1) + S + 2×(n(n-1)/2 - (n-1) - S) where S = Σ(C(deg(w),2))
= (n-1) + S + (n(n-1) - 2(n-1) - 2S)
= (n-1) + S + n(n-1) - 2(n-1) - 2S
= n(n-1) - (n-1) - S
= (n-1)(n-1) - S
= (n-1)² - S

Where S = Σ(degree(w)×(degree(w)-1)/2) for all nodes w

Let me verify with examples:

Example 1: n=4, degrees: [3,1,1,1]
S = 3×2/2 + 3×0/2 = 3
Sum = (4-1)² - 3 = 9 - 3 = 6 ✓

Example 2: n=4, degrees: [1,2,2,1]
S = 1×0/2 + 2×1/2 + 2×1/2 + 1×0/2 = 0 + 1 + 1 + 0 = 2
Sum = (4-1)² - 2 = 9 - 2 = 7 ✓

Perfect! This matches the examples.

## Final Solution

```python
n = int(input())
if n == 2:
    print(1)
else:
    # Build adjacency list
    adj = [[] for _ in range(n + 1)]
    
    for _ in range(n - 1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        adj[u].append(v)
        adj[v].append(u)
    
    # Calculate sum of C(degree(w), 2) for all nodes w
    S = 0
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        deg = len(adj[i])
        S += deg * (deg - 1) // 2
    
    # Sum of distances = (n-1)^2 - S
    result = (n - 1) * (n - 1) - S
    print(result)
```

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a tree with `n` nodes (subway stations) and `n-1` edges (tunnels)
2. We add new tunnels between any two stations that share a common neighbor in the original tree
3. We need to find the sum of pairwise distances in the new graph

## Key Insights

1. In the original tree, we have a tree structure where each pair has exactly one path
2. When we add edges between nodes that share a common neighbor, we're essentially creating a new graph
3. Nodes that share a common neighbor will now be directly connected
4. This operation will create a graph where many pairs of nodes become directly connected

## Analysis of the Examples

**Example 1:** Tree is a star with center at node 1 connected to nodes 2, 3, 4
- After adding edges: all pairs are directly connected (complete graph)
- Distances: (1,2)=1, (1,3)=1, (1,4)=1, (2,3)=1, (2,4)=1, (3,4)=1
- Sum = 6

**Example 2:** Tree is a path 1-2-3-4
- Nodes sharing common neighbors:
  - 1 and 3: share neighbor 2
  - 2 and 4: share neighbor 3
  - 1 and 2: already connected
  - 2 and 3: already connected  
  - 3 and 4: already connected
- So we add edges (1,3) and (2,4)

- Distance (1,4): path 1-3-4 or 1-2-4, so distance = 2
- All other pairs have distance 1
- Sum = 1+1+1+1+1+2 = 7

```python
def solve():
    n = int(input())
    if n == 2:
        print(1)
        return
    
    # Build adjacency list
    adj = [[] for _ in range(n + 1)]
    
    for _ in range(n - 1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        adj[u].append(v)
        adj[v].append(u)
    
    # Count pairs with distance 1 in new graph
    # Distance 1 pairs: original edges and new edges (pairs sharing common neighbor)
    
    # Original edges
    original_edges = n - 1
    
    # New edges: pairs that share a common neighbor
    new_edges = 0
    for u in range(1, n + 1):
        # For each neighbor of u, and for each other neighbor of u
        neighbors = adj[u]
        k = len(neighbors)
        # Pairs among neighbors of u
        new_edges += k * (k - 1) // 2 - (k - 1)  # This is wrong
    
    # Actually, for each node u, the pairs among its neighbors all share u as common neighbor
    # So total new edges = sum over all u of (number of pairs among neighbors of u)
    # But each pair (v1,v2) that shares a common neighbor is counted once for each common neighbor
    # Actually, if v1 and v2 share exactly one common neighbor, they are counted once
    # But they might share multiple common neighbors? In a tree, if v1 and v2 have a common neighbor w,
    # they might have multiple common neighbors?
    
    # Actually, if v1 and v2 have a common neighbor w, and w is unique (in trees), then they share exactly one common neighbor.
    # But in a tree, if v1 and v2 have a common neighbor w, and v1 and v2 are not adjacent, they might have only one common neighbor.
    # Actually, in trees, two nodes can have at most one common neighbor.
    
    # So new edges = sum over all nodes u of (number of pairs of neighbors of u)
    new_edges = 0
    for u in range(1, n + 1):
        neighbors = adj[u]
        k = len(neighbors)
        new_edges += k * (k - 1) // 2
    
    # Total pairs with distance 1 = original edges + new edges
    dist_1_pairs = original_edges + new_edges
    
    # Total pairs = n * (n - 1) // 2
    total_pairs = n * (n - 1) // 2
    
    # Pairs with distance 2 = total pairs - pairs with distance 1
    dist_2_pairs = total_pairs - dist_1_pairs
    
    # Answer = sum of distances = 1 * dist_1_pairs + 2 * dist_2_pairs
    result = dist_1_pairs + 2 * dist_2_pairs
    print(result)

solve()
```

Actually, if two nodes v1 and v2 share a common neighbor w, then they are counted in the sum when we consider node w. 

But in the tree, two nodes can share at most one common neighbor? Actually, this is not true in general - two nodes can share multiple common neighbors in a tree? 

No, in a tree, if two nodes have the same neighbor w, and if there's another neighbor w', then the path from v1 to w to w' to v2 would create a cycle, which is impossible in a tree. 

So in a tree, any two nodes have at most one common neighbor.

Therefore, each pair of nodes that share a common neighbor is counted exactly once in our sum.

So the total number of pairs with distance 1 is exactly the sum over all nodes u of (number of pairs of neighbors of u).

The total number of pairs with distance 2 is total_pairs - distance_1_pairs.

```python
n = int(input())

if n == 2:
    print(1)
else:
    # Build adjacency list
    adj = [[] for _ in range(n + 1)]
    
    for _ in range(n - 1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        adj[u].append(v)
        adj[v].append(u)
    
    # Count pairs that share a common neighbor (distance 1 in new graph)
    pairs_with_distance_1 = 0
    for u in range(1, n + 1):
        neighbors = adj[u]
        k = len(neighbors)
        # Number of pairs among these neighbors
        pairs_with_distance_1 += k * (k - 1) // 2
    
    # Total number of pairs
    total_pairs = n * (n - 1) // 2
    
    # Number of pairs with distance 2
    pairs_with_distance_2 = total_pairs - pairs_with_distance_1
    
    # Answer = sum of distances
    result = pairs_with_distance_1 * 1 + pairs_with_distance_2 * 2
    print(result)
```

It is guaranteed that these $n$ stations and $n - 1$ tunnels form a tree.

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    if n == 2:
        print(1)
        return
    
    # Build adjacency list for original tree
    adj = defaultdict(list)
    for _ in range(n - 1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        adj[u].append(v)
        adj[v].append(u)
    
    # Build new adjacency list - original edges + edges between neighbors of same node
    new_adj = defaultdict(list)
    
    # Add original edges
    for u in adj:
        for v in adj[u]:
            new_adj[u].append(v)
            new_adj[v].append(u)
    
    # Add edges between nodes that share a common neighbor
    for w in adj:
        neighbors = adj[w]
        # Add edge between every pair of neighbors
        for i in range(len(neighbors)):
            for j in range(i + 1, len(neighbors)):
                u, v = neighbors[i], neighbors[j]
                new_adj[u].append(v)
                new_adj[v].append(u)
    
    # Calculate sum of distances between all pairs
    total_distance = 0
    
    # For each pair (i, j) with i < j
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        for j in range(i + 1, n + 1):
            # BFS to find shortest distance from i to j
            dist = [-1] * (n + 1)
            dist[i] = 0
            queue = deque([i])
            
            while queue:
                node = queue.popleft()
                if node == j:
                    break
                for neighbor in new_adj[node]:
                    if dist[neighbor] == -1:
                        dist[neighbor] = dist[node] + 1
                        queue.append(neighbor)
            
            total_distance += dist[j]
    
    print(total_distance)

solve()
```

Number of edges = n-1

So A = (n-1) + sum over all w of C(|N(w)|, 2)

For pairs with distance ≥ 3, I need the sum of original distances minus the sum for distance ≤ 2.

Total sum of all pairwise distances in a tree can be computed efficiently.

Let me compute total pairwise sum first, then subtract the sum for distance ≤ 2 and add back (distance - 1) for each distance ≥ 3.

Standard formula: sum of distances over all pairs in a tree can be computed using DFS to compute subtree sizes.

Sum of all pairwise distances = sum over all edges of (size of subtree on one side * (n - size of subtree on other side))

For distance ≤ 2 part, we already have count A.

For distance ≥ 3 part, we need sum of distances for pairs with distance ≥ 3 = (total sum) - (sum for distance ≤ 2)

But sum for distance ≤ 2 is not just A, it's the sum of distances for those pairs.

For distance 1 pairs: there are (n-1) pairs, each contributes 1 → sum = n-1
For distance 2 pairs: each contributes 2, and there are sum_w C(|N(w)|, 2) such pairs → sum = 2 * sum_w C(|N(w)|, 2)

So sum for distance ≤ 2 = (n-1) + 2 * sum_w C(|N(w)|, 2)

Sum for distance ≥ 3 = (total sum of all pairwise distances) - ((n-1) + 2 * sum_w C(|N(w)|, 2))

Final answer = A + (sum for distance ≥ 3 - C) 
where A = (n-1) + sum_w C(|N(w)|, 2)  (number of pairs with dist ≤ 2)
and C = (total pairs) - A = C(n,2) - A

Answer = A + (sum for distance ≥ 3 - C) = A + (total_sum - (n-1 + 2*sum_w C(|N(w)|,2)) - (C(n,2) - A))
= A + total_sum - (n-1 + 2*sum_w C(|N(w)|,2)) - (n(n-1)/2 - A)
= 2A + total_sum - (n-1 + 2*sum_w C(|N(w)|,2)) - n(n-1)/2

A = (n-1) + sum_w C(|N(w)|, 2)

So:

Final answer = 2(n-1) + 2*sum_w C(|N(w)|, 2) + total_sum - (n-1 + 2*sum_w C(|N(w)|,2)) - n(n-1)/2
= (2(n-1) - (n-1)) + total_sum - n(n-1)/2
= (n-1) + total_sum - n(n-1)/2

= 2*C2 + T - S2 - C(n,2)

= 2(n-1) + 2*D2 + T - (n-1) - 2*D2 - n*(n-1)/2

Example 2: n=4, path 1-2-3-4

Distances:
(1,2):1, (1,3):2, (1,4):3, (2,3):1, (2,4):2, (3,4):1

Answer = 10 + (4-1)(2-4)/2 = 10 + 3*(-2)/2 = 10 - 3 = 7 ✓

Perfect! It works.

So algorithm:
1. Compute total sum of pairwise distances T in the original tree
2. Compute answer = T + (n-1)*(2-n)/2

To compute T (sum of all pairwise distances in a tree), we can use the standard method:
- For each edge (u,v), let s be the size of the subtree on one side when we remove the edge, then contribution is s * (n-s)
- Sum over all edges

```python
from collections import defaultdict

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    
    if n == 2:
        print(1)
        return
    
    # Build adjacency list
    adj = defaultdict(list)
    edges = []
    
    for _ in range(n - 1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        adj[u].append(v)
        adj[v].append(u)
        edges.append((u, v))
    
    # Compute total sum of pairwise distances in tree
    total_sum = 0
    
    # For each edge, compute the size of the subtree when removing the edge
    # We'll do DFS from node 1
    parent = [-1] * (n + 1)
    size = [0] * (n + 1)
    
    def dfs(u, p):
        parent[u] = p
        size[u] = 1
        for v in adj[u]:
            if v != p:
                dfs(v, u)
                size[u] += size[v]
    
    dfs(1, -1)
    
    # For each edge (u,v), contribution is size[u] * (n - size[u])
    # But we need to be careful about which side is smaller
    for u, v in edges:
        # When removing edge (u,v), one side has size[u] (if u is parent of v) or size[v]
        # Actually, when we do DFS from 1, v is child of u if u is parent of v
        if parent[v] == u:
            # v is child of u, size of subtree is size[v]
            total_sum += size[v] * (n - size[v])
        else:
            # u is child of v, size of subtree is size[u]
            total_sum += size[u] * (n - size[u])
    
    # The formula for answer is total_sum + (n-1)*(2-n)/2
    answer = total_sum + (n - 1) * (2 - n) // 2
    
    print(answer)

solve()
```

Wait, let me verify the edge contribution. In the formula, for an edge, the contribution to total pairwise distance is size * (n-size), where size is the size of the connected component when we remove the edge.

In the DFS above, when we have parent[v] == u, then when removing edge (u,v), the component containing v has size = size[v], and the other has size = n - size[v].

Yes, that's correct.

For the examples:
Example 1 (star): edges (1,2), (1,3), (1,4)
- When removing (1,2): size[2]=1, contribution = 1*(4-1)=3
- When removing (1,3): size[3]=1, contribution = 1*3=3  
- When removing (1,4): size[4]=1, contribution = 1*3=3
- Total T = 3+3+3 = 9 ✓

Example 2 (path 1-2-3-4):
- Edges: (1,2), (2,3), (3,4)
- DFS from 1: 
  - size[4]=1, size[3]=2 (child of 4), size[2]=3 (child of 3), size[1]=4
- Removing (1,2): v=2, parent[2]=1, size[2]=3, contribution = 3*(4-3)=3
- Removing (2,3): v=3, parent[3]=2, size[3]=2, contribution = 2*(4-2)=4
- Removing (3,4): v=4, parent[4]=3, size[4]=1, contribution = 1*(4-1)=3
- Total T = 3+4+3 = 10 ✓

Perfect.

Final code:

```python
from collections import defaultdict

n = int(input())

if n == 2:
    print(1)
else:
    adj = defaultdict(list)
    edges = []
    
    for _ in range(n - 1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        adj[u].append(v)
        adj[v].append(u)
        edges.append((u, v))
    
    # Compute total sum of pairwise distances in tree
    parent = [-1] * (n + 1)
    size = [0] * (n + 1)
    
    def dfs(u, p):
        parent[u] = p
        size[u] = 1
        for v in adj[u]:
            if v != p:
                dfs(v, u)
                size[u] += size[v]
    
    dfs(1, -1)
    
    # For each edge, contribution is size * (n - size) where size is size of smaller component
    total_sum = 0
    for u, v in edges:
        if parent[v] == u:
            total_sum += size[v] * (n - size[v])
        else:
            total_sum += size[u] * (n - size[u])
    
    answer = total_sum + (n - 1) * (2 - n) // 2
    print(answer)
```

Submitted Code

View Extracted Code 
from collections import defaultdict

n = int(input())

if n == 2:
    print(1)
else:
    adj = defaultdict(list)
    edges = []
    
    for _ in range(n - 1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        adj[u].append(v)
        adj[v].append(u)
        edges.append((u, v))
    
    # Compute total sum of pairwise distances in tree
    parent = [-1] * (n + 1)
    size = [0] * (n + 1)
    
    def dfs(u, p):
        parent[u] = p
        size[u] = 1
        for v in adj[u]:
            if v != p:
                dfs(v, u)
                size[u] += size[v]
    
    dfs(1, -1)
    
    # For each edge, contribution is size * (n - size) where size is size of smaller component
    total_sum = 0
    for u, v in edges:
        if parent[v] == u:
            total_sum += size[v] * (n - size[v])
        else:
            total_sum += size[u] * (n - size[u])
    
    answer = total_sum + (n - 1) * (2 - n) // 2
    print(answer)

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Sergey Semyonovich is a mayor of a county city N and he used to spend his days and nights in thoughts of further improvements of Nkers' lives. Unfortunately for him, anything and everything has been done already, and there are no more possible improvements he can think of during the day (he now prefers to sleep at night). However, his assistants have found a solution and they now draw an imaginary city on a paper sheet and suggest the mayor can propose its improvements.

Right now he has a map of some imaginary city with $n$ subway stations. Some stations are directly connected with tunnels in such a way that the whole map is a tree (assistants were short on time and enthusiasm). It means that there exists exactly one simple path between each pair of station. We call a path simple if it uses each tunnel no more than once.

One of Sergey Semyonovich's favorite quality objectives is the sum of all pairwise distances between every pair of stations. The distance between two stations is the minimum possible number of tunnels on a path between them.

Sergey Semyonovich decided to add new tunnels to the subway map. In particular, he connected any two stations $u$ and $v$ that were not connected with a direct tunnel but share a common neighbor, i.e. there exists such a station $w$ that the original map has a tunnel between $u$ and $w$ and a tunnel between $w$ and $v$. You are given a task to compute the sum of pairwise distances between all pairs of stations in the new map.


-----Input-----

The first line of the input contains a single integer $n$ ($2 \leq n \leq 200\,000$) — the number of subway stations in the imaginary city drawn by mayor's assistants. Each of the following $n - 1$ lines contains two integers $u_i$ and $v_i$ ($1 \leq u_i, v_i \leq n$, $u_i \ne v_i$), meaning the station with these indices are connected with a direct tunnel.

It is guaranteed that these $n$ stations and $n - 1$ tunnels form a tree.


-----Output-----

Print one integer that is equal to the sum of distances between all pairs of stations after Sergey Semyonovich draws new tunnels between all pairs of stations that share a common neighbor in the original map.


-----Examples-----
Input
4
1 2
1 3
1 4

Output
6

Input
4
1 2
2 3
3 4

Output
7



-----Note-----

In the first sample, in the new map all pairs of stations share a direct connection, so the sum of distances is $6$.

In the second sample, the new map has a direct tunnel between all pairs of stations except for the pair $(1, 4)$. For these two stations the distance is $2$.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a tree with n nodes (subway stations) and n-1 edges (tunnels)
2. We add new tunnels between any two stations that share a common neighbor in the original tree
3. We need to find the sum of all pairwise distances in the new graph

## Key Insights

1. In the original tree, two nodes share a common neighbor if they are both connected to the same node
2. After adding edges between nodes that share common neighbors, we create a new graph
3. The resulting graph will have certain properties based on the original tree structure

## Analysis of Examples

**Example 1**: Star graph (node 1 connected to 2, 3, 4)
- Original: 1-2, 1-3, 1-4
- Nodes 2,3,4 all share neighbor 1
- So we add edges between 2-3, 2-4, 3-4
- Now all pairs are directly connected (complete graph K4)
- Distance between any two nodes is 1
- Sum = 1+1+1+1+1+1 = 6 ✓

**Example 2**: Path graph (1-2-3-4)
- Original: 1-2, 2-3, 3-4
- Common neighbors:
  - 1 and 3 share neighbor 2
  - 2 and 4 share neighbor 3
- So we add edges 1-3 and 2-4
- New edges: 1-2, 2-3, 3-4, 1-3, 2-4
- Distances:
  - (1,2): 1
  - (1,3): 1
  - (1,4): 2 (1→3→4 or 1→2→4)
  - (2,3): 1
  - (2,4): 1
  - (3,4): 1
- Sum = 1+1+2+1+1+1 = 7 ✓

## General Solution Approach

After adding edges between nodes that share common neighbors, we need to:
1. Identify which pairs of nodes will be directly connected
2. Calculate the distance between each pair in the resulting graph

However, there's a pattern: nodes that don't share a common neighbor will have distance 2, while others will have distance 1.

For any two nodes that share a common neighbor in the original tree, they become directly connected.

For any two nodes that don't share a common neighbor, the distance becomes 2 (since they can reach each other through their common neighbor).

But wait - let's think about when two nodes don't share a common neighbor:
- Two nodes share a common neighbor if there exists a node w such that w is adjacent to both
- Otherwise, their distance is at least 2

Actually, let me reconsider: in the resulting graph, two nodes will have distance 1 if they share a common neighbor in the original tree, otherwise they will have distance 2 (since they can go through their common neighbor).

Wait, that's not quite right either. Let me think differently.

## Final Insight

After adding edges between nodes that share a common neighbor:
- If two nodes share a common neighbor in the original tree, they become directly connected (distance 1)
- Otherwise, they will have distance 2 in the new graph (since they can go through their common neighbor)

But is this always true? Let me verify with the examples.

Actually, there's a simpler approach: we can calculate the sum of distances by considering the contribution of each edge.

However, given the constraints and the examples, the pattern is:
- For each pair of nodes, if they share a common neighbor in the original tree, distance is 1
- Otherwise, distance is 2

But looking at the path example: nodes 1 and 4 don't share a common neighbor, so distance should be 2, which matches.

So the solution is:
1. Count pairs that share a common neighbor (distance = 1)
2. Count pairs that don't share a common neighbor (distance = 2)
3. Sum = (number of pairs sharing common neighbor) × 1 + (number of pairs not sharing common neighbor) × 2

Total pairs = n*(n-1)/2

Let me implement this:

```python
from collections import defaultdict

n = int(input())
if n == 2:
    print(1)
else:
    # Build adjacency list
    adj = defaultdict(list)
    for _ in range(n - 1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        adj[u].append(v)
        adj[v].append(u)
    
    # Count pairs that share a common neighbor
    common_neighbors_pairs = 0
    
    # For each node, count how many pairs of its neighbors it creates
    for node in range(1, n + 1):
        neighbors = adj[node]
        k = len(neighbors)
        if k >= 2:
            # Each pair of neighbors shares this node as common neighbor
            # Number of pairs = k * (k - 1) / 2
            common_neighbors_pairs += k * (k - 1) // 2
    
    # Total pairs
    total_pairs = n * (n - 1) // 2
    
    # Pairs that don't share common neighbor
    no_common_neighbors_pairs = total_pairs - common_neighbors_pairs
    
    # Sum of distances = pairs with distance 1 + pairs with distance 2
    result = common_neighbors_pairs * 1 + no_common_neighbors_pairs * 2
    
    print(result)
```

- Node 1 has 3 neighbors → 3*(3-1)/2 = 3 pairs that share a common neighbor
- But actually, any pair of nodes (2,3), (2,4), (3,4) all share node 1
- So all 6 pairs share a common neighbor?

- Node 1: neighbors [2] → 0 pairs
- Node 2: neighbors [1,3] → 1 pair (1,3) 
- Node 3: neighbors [2,4] → 1 pair (2,4)
- Node 4: neighbors [3] → 0 pairs

Pairs that have distance 2: (1,4) → 1 pair
Sum = 5*1 + 1*2 = 7 ✓

The pairs that don't share a common neighbor have distance 2.

In path 1-2-3-4:
- (1,3): share neighbor 2 → distance 1
- (2,4): share neighbor 3 → distance 1
- (1,4): don't share common neighbor → distance 2

So we need to count pairs that do NOT share a common neighbor.

I need to reconsider.

Actually, looking at the examples again:
- In example 1, all pairs become directly connected (complete graph) after adding edges
- In example 2, we add edges 1-3 and 2-4, so (1,4) has distance 2

So the distance between two nodes in the new graph is:
- 1 if they were already directly connected (original edge) OR if they share a common neighbor (new edge)
- 2 if they are not directly connected and don't share a common neighbor

No, that's not quite right either. If they share a common neighbor, they get a direct connection.

So distance = 1 if there's a direct connection (original or new), 2 otherwise.

In path example:
- (1,2): original edge → distance 1
- (1,3): new edge (share neighbor 2) → distance 1  
- (1,4): no direct edge, no common neighbor → distance 2
- (2,3): original edge → distance 1
- (2,4): new edge (share neighbor 3) → distance 1
- (3,4): original edge → distance 1

Sum = 1+1+2+1+1+1 = 7 ✓

So the distance is 1 if there's a direct path (original or new), 2 otherwise.

In example 1 (star):
- Original edges: 1-2, 1-3, 1-4
- New edges: 2-3, 2-4, 3-4 (since all pairs share neighbor 1)
- So all pairs have distance 1
- Sum = 6 ✓

So algorithm:
1. Start with original edges (distance 1)
2. Add edges between pairs that share common neighbor in original tree (distance 1)
3. For remaining pairs, distance is 2

```python
from collections import defaultdict

n = int(input())
if n == 2:
    print(1)
else:
    # Build adjacency list
    adj = defaultdict(list)
    edges = []
    for _ in range(n - 1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        adj[u].append(v)
        adj[v].append(u)
        edges.append((u, v))
    
    # Find all pairs that share a common neighbor
    new_edges = set()
    for node in range(1, n + 1):
        neighbors = adj[node]
        k = len(neighbors)
        if k >= 2:
            for i in range(k):
                for j in range(i + 1, k):
                    u, v = neighbors[i], neighbors[j]
                    new_edges.add((min(u, v), max(u, v)))
    
    # Total pairs
    total_pairs = n * (n - 1) // 2
    
    # Count pairs with distance 1
    distance_1_pairs = 0
    
    # Original edges
    for u, v in edges:
        distance_1_pairs += 1
    
    # New edges
    distance_1_pairs += len(new_edges)
    
    # Pairs with distance 2
    distance_2_pairs = total_pairs - distance_1_pairs
    
    result = distance_1_pairs * 1 + distance_2_pairs * 2
    print(result)
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a tree with n nodes (subway stations)
2. We add new edges between any two nodes that share a common neighbor in the original tree
3. We need to find the sum of all pairwise distances in the new graph

## Key Insights

1. In the original tree, any two nodes that don't share a common neighbor won't get a direct edge
2. When we add edges between nodes that share a common neighbor, we're essentially making certain parts of the tree more connected
3. The key observation is that after adding these edges, the graph becomes "almost complete" - missing only edges that don't share a common neighbor

## Analysis of Examples

**Example 1**: Star graph (center 1 connected to 2,3,4)
- In original: distances are 1 (all pairs through center)
- After adding edges: all pairs become directly connected
- Sum of distances = 1+1+1+1+1+1 = 6

**Example 2**: Path graph (1-2-3-4)
- Original connections: 1-2, 2-3, 3-4
- Nodes sharing common neighbors:
  - 1 and 3: both connected to 2 → add edge (1,3)
  - 2 and 4: both connected to 3 → add edge (2,4)
  - 1 and 4: no common neighbor → no edge
  - 1 and 2: already connected
  - 2 and 3: already connected  
  - 3 and 4: already connected
- New edges: (1,3), (2,4)
- Distances:
  - (1,2): 1
  - (1,3): 1
  - (1,4): 2 (path 1-2-4 or 1-3-4)
  - (2,3): 1
  - (2,4): 1
  - (3,4): 1
- Sum = 1+1+2+1+1+1 = 7

## General Solution Approach

1. Build the original tree from input
2. For each node, identify all nodes that share a common neighbor with it
3. The resulting graph will have direct edges between nodes that share a common neighbor
4. The distance between any two nodes will be at most 2 (since any two nodes either share a common neighbor or are connected through a path of length 2)

Wait, let me reconsider. In the new graph:

- Two nodes are directly connected if they share a common neighbor
- For any two nodes that don't share a common neighbor, their distance might be 2
- Actually, in the examples, we see that only pairs that don't share a common neighbor have distance > 1

Let me think about when two nodes don't share a common neighbor:
- In a path graph 1-2-3-4: nodes 1 and 4 don't share a common neighbor
- In a star graph: all pairs share a common neighbor (the center)

The distance between any two nodes in the new graph:
- If they share a common neighbor → distance = 1
- Otherwise → distance = 2 (through their common neighbor via the path)

For any two nodes u,v:
- If there exists w such that u-w and v-w are edges → distance = 1
- Otherwise → distance = 2

So the sum of distances = (number of pairs with distance 1) + 2 × (number of pairs with distance 2)

We need to count pairs that don't share a common neighbor.

Let me implement this:

```python
def solve():
    n = int(input())
    
    if n == 2:
        print(1)
        return
    
    # Build adjacency list
    adj = [[] for _ in range(n + 1)]
    
    for _ in range(n - 1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        adj[u].append(v)
        adj[v].append(u)
    
    # Count pairs that share a common neighbor
    # For each node, count how many neighbors it has
    # Two nodes u, v share a common neighbor if there exists w such that w is neighbor of both u and v
    
    # For each node w, it creates edges between all pairs of its neighbors
    pairs_with_distance_1 = 0
    
    for w in range(1, n + 1):
        neighbors = adj[w]
        if len(neighbors) >= 2:
            # For each pair of neighbors of w, they now have distance 1
            # Number of pairs = C(k,2) where k = len(neighbors)
            k = len(neighbors)
            pairs_with_distance_1 += k * (k - 1) // 2
    
    # Total pairs is n*(n-1)//2
    total_pairs = n * (n - 1) // 2
    
    # Pairs with distance 2 = total pairs - pairs with distance 1
    pairs_with_distance_2 = total_pairs - pairs_with_distance_1
    
    # Sum of distances = sum of distance 1 pairs + 2 * sum of distance 2 pairs
    result = pairs_with_distance_1 * 1 + pairs_with_distance_2 * 2
    
    print(result)

solve()
```

In the star example:
- Node 1 has neighbors [2,3,4], so pairs (2,3), (2,4), (3,4) have distance 1

For any two nodes u,v:

- In a star: if u and v are both leaves, they share the center and other leaves
- If one is center and one is leaf: they share other leaves as common neighbors

In path graph 1-2-3-4:
- Pair (1,4): 1's neighbors [2], 4's neighbors [3]. Common neighbors? 2 and 3 are not in both. So no common neighbor → distance = 2
- All other pairs have distance 1

So my approach is correct.

For star example: all pairs have distance 1, so sum = 6 (correct)

For path example: 5 pairs have distance 1, 1 pair has distance 2, sum = 5*1 + 1*2 = 7 (correct)

```python
n = int(input())

if n == 2:
    print(1)
else:
    # Build adjacency list
    adj = [[] for _ in range(n + 1)]
    
    for _ in range(n - 1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        adj[u].append(v)
        adj[v].append(u)
    
    # Count pairs that share a common neighbor (distance 1)
    pairs_with_distance_1 = 0
    
    for w in range(1, n + 1):
        neighbors = adj[w]
        k = len(neighbors)
        if k >= 2:
            # All pairs of neighbors of w will have distance 1
            pairs_with_distance_1 += k * (k - 1) // 2
    
    # Total number of pairs
    total_pairs = n * (n - 1) // 2
    
    # Pairs with distance 2 = total pairs - pairs with distance 1
    pairs_with_distance_2 = total_pairs - pairs_with_distance_1
    
    # Sum of distances = sum of distance 1 pairs + 2 * sum of distance 2 pairs
    result = pairs_with_distance_1 * 1 + pairs_with_distance_2 * 2
    
    print(result)
```

Submitted Code

View Extracted Code 
n = int(input())

if n == 2:
    print(1)
else:
    # Build adjacency list
    adj = [[] for _ in range(n + 1)]
    
    for _ in range(n - 1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        adj[u].append(v)
        adj[v].append(u)
    
    # Count pairs that share a common neighbor (distance 1)
    pairs_with_distance_1 = 0
    
    for w in range(1, n + 1):
        neighbors = adj[w]
        k = len(neighbors)
        if k >= 2:
            # All pairs of neighbors of w will have distance 1
            pairs_with_distance_1 += k * (k - 1) // 2
    
    # Total number of pairs
    total_pairs = n * (n - 1) // 2
    
    # Pairs with distance 2 = total pairs - pairs with distance 1
    pairs_with_distance_2 = total_pairs - pairs_with_distance_1
    
    # Sum of distances = sum of distance 1 pairs + 2 * sum of distance 2 pairs
    result = pairs_with_distance_1 * 1 + pairs_with_distance_2 * 2
    
    print(result)

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Sergey Semyonovich is a mayor of a county city N and he used to spend his days and nights in thoughts of further improvements of Nkers' lives. Unfortunately for him, anything and everything has been done already, and there are no more possible improvements he can think of during the day (he now prefers to sleep at night). However, his assistants have found a solution and they now draw an imaginary city on a paper sheet and suggest the mayor can propose its improvements.

Right now he has a map of some imaginary city with $n$ subway stations. Some stations are directly connected with tunnels in such a way that the whole map is a tree (assistants were short on time and enthusiasm). It means that there exists exactly one simple path between each pair of station. We call a path simple if it uses each tunnel no more than once.

One of Sergey Semyonovich's favorite quality objectives is the sum of all pairwise distances between every pair of stations. The distance between two stations is the minimum possible number of tunnels on a path between them.

Sergey Semyonovich decided to add new tunnels to the subway map. In particular, he connected any two stations $u$ and $v$ that were not connected with a direct tunnel but share a common neighbor, i.e. there exists such a station $w$ that the original map has a tunnel between $u$ and $w$ and a tunnel between $w$ and $v$. You are given a task to compute the sum of pairwise distances between all pairs of stations in the new map.


-----Input-----

The first line of the input contains a single integer $n$ ($2 \leq n \leq 200\,000$) — the number of subway stations in the imaginary city drawn by mayor's assistants. Each of the following $n - 1$ lines contains two integers $u_i$ and $v_i$ ($1 \leq u_i, v_i \leq n$, $u_i \ne v_i$), meaning the station with these indices are connected with a direct tunnel.

It is guaranteed that these $n$ stations and $n - 1$ tunnels form a tree.


-----Output-----

Print one integer that is equal to the sum of distances between all pairs of stations after Sergey Semyonovich draws new tunnels between all pairs of stations that share a common neighbor in the original map.


-----Examples-----
Input
4
1 2
1 3
1 4

Output
6

Input
4
1 2
2 3
3 4

Output
7



-----Note-----

In the first sample, in the new map all pairs of stations share a direct connection, so the sum of distances is $6$.

In the second sample, the new map has a direct tunnel between all pairs of stations except for the pair $(1, 4)$. View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a tree with n nodes (subway stations)
2. We add new edges between any two nodes that share a common neighbor in the original tree
3. We need to find the sum of all pairwise distances in the new graph

## Key Insights

1. In the original tree, we have n-1 edges
2. We add edges between any two nodes that have a common neighbor
3. After adding these edges, what do we get?

Let me analyze the examples:

**Example 1**: Star graph with center 1 connected to 2,3,4
- Nodes 2,3,4 all share neighbor 1
- So we add edges (2,3), (2,4), (3,4)
- Now all nodes are directly connected (complete graph)
- Distance between any pair is 1
- Sum = 1+1+1+1+1+1 = 6 ✓

**Example 2**: Path 1-2-3-4
- Nodes that share a common neighbor:
  - 1 and 2 share neighbor 2? No, 1 only connects to 2
  - 1 and 3 share neighbor 2? Yes (1-2-3)
  - 1 and 4 share neighbor 2? No
  - 2 and 3 share neighbor 3? Yes (2-3-3) - wait, let me reconsider
- Actually: 
  - 1 and 3 share neighbor 2 (path 1-2-3)
  - 2 and 4 share neighbor 3 (path 2-3-4)
  - 1 and 4 don't share a common neighbor
- So we add edges (1,3) and (2,4)
- New graph: edges (1,2), (2,3), (3,4), (1,3), (2,4)
- Distance (1,4): path 1-3-4 = 2, or 1-2-4 = 2
- All other pairs: distance 1
- Sum = 1+1+1+1+1+2 = 7 ✓

## General Pattern

After adding edges between nodes that share a common neighbor, what happens?

Nodes that share a common neighbor become directly connected. This means that for any node v, all nodes at distance 2 from v become connected to each other.

Actually, let me think about connected components:

- If a node v has neighbors u1, u2, ..., uk, then in the new graph, all pairs among these neighbors are directly connected
- So the subgraph induced by the neighbors of any node becomes a clique (complete graph)

This means that the entire graph becomes connected, but with different distances.

## Algorithm

The key insight is that after adding these edges, the graph has a special structure. We can think about it this way:

For any pair of nodes (u,v), their distance in the new graph is at most 2, because:
- If u and v are adjacent in original tree (distance 1), distance remains 1
- If u and v are at distance 2 in original tree (share a common neighbor w), they get a direct edge (distance 1)
- If u and v are at distance 3 in original tree, they might have distance 2 in new graph

Wait, let me reconsider the distance calculation:

For any pair of nodes (u,v):
- If they are directly connected in original tree → distance 1 in new graph
- If they share a common neighbor in original tree → distance 1 in new graph  
- Otherwise (distance ≥ 3 in original tree) → distance 2 in new graph

Is this always true?

Actually, in the path example (1-2-3-4):
- (1,4) have distance 3 in original, distance 2 in new (through 3 or through 2)
- All pairs with distance 1 or 2 in original have distance 1 in new
- Only pairs at distance 3 have distance 2 in new

For the star example:
- All pairs had distance 2 in original (since star has diameter 2)
- After adding edges, all pairs have distance 1

So the pattern seems to be: in the new graph, distance between any two nodes is at most 2.

Therefore:
- Distance = 1 if the nodes are adjacent in original tree OR share a common neighbor in original tree
- Distance = 2 otherwise

But "share a common neighbor" means they're at distance 2 in original tree.

So:
- Distance = 1 if original distance ≤ 2
- Distance = 2 if original distance ≥ 3

Wait, no - in original tree, we add edges between nodes at distance 2. So in new graph:
- Distance = 1 if original distance ≤ 2
- Distance = 2 if original distance ≥ 3

Yes! This matches both examples.

## Solution Steps

1. Build the tree from input
2. Compute the distance between every pair of nodes in the original tree (but we don't need exact values, we need to count how many pairs have distance 1, 2, 3, etc.)
3. Sum = (number of pairs with distance 1) × 1 + (number of pairs with distance 2) × 1 + (number of pairs with distance ≥ 3) × 2

Wait, actually: in the new graph:
- If original distance ≤ 2 → new distance = 1
- If original distance ≥ 3 → new distance = 2

So sum = (pairs with dist ≤ 2) × 1 + (pairs with dist ≥ 3) × 2

But we can compute this easily:

Total pairs = n*(n-1)/2
Pairs with dist 1 = edges in original tree = n-1
Pairs with dist 2 = pairs at distance 2 in original tree
Pairs with dist ≥ 3 = total pairs - pairs with dist 1 - pairs with dist 2

Sum = (n-1) + (pairs at dist 2) + 2 * (total pairs - (n-1) - pairs at dist 2)

Simplifying: = (n-1) + (pairs at dist 2) + 2 * (total pairs - (n-1) - pairs at dist 2)
= (n-1) + (pairs at dist 2) + 2*(total pairs - (n-1)) - 2*(pairs at dist 2)
= (n-1) + 2*(total pairs - (n-1)) - (pairs at dist 2)
= (n-1) + 2*total_pairs - 2*(n-1) - pairs at dist 2
= 2*total_pairs - (n-1) - pairs at dist 2

So we need to count pairs at distance 2 in the original tree.

We can do this by:
1. For each node i, count how many of its neighbors are connected to other nodes at distance 2
2. But we need to avoid double counting

Actually, for each node v, let d_v be the number of neighbors of v. Then for each pair of neighbors u,w of v (u ≠ w), the pair (u,w) has distance 2 in original tree.

So pairs at distance 2 = sum over all nodes v of C(d_v, 2)

## Implementation

```python
n = int(input())

if n == 2:
    print(1)
else:
    # Build adjacency list
    adj = [[] for _ in range(n+1)]
    
    for _ in range(n-1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        adj[u].append(v)
        adj[v].append(u)
    
    # Count pairs at distance 2
    pairs_dist_2 = 0
    for i in range(1, n+1):
        degree = len(adj[i])
        pairs_dist_2 += degree * (degree - 1) // 2
    
    total_pairs = n * (n - 1) // 2
    
    # Sum of distances in new graph
    # Pairs with distance 1: n-1 (original edges)
    # Pairs with distance 2: pairs_dist_2
    # Pairs with distance >=3: total_pairs - (n-1) - pairs_dist_2
    # Sum = (n-1) + pairs_dist_2 + 2 * (total_pairs - (n-1) - pairs_dist_2)
    
    result = (n - 1) + pairs_dist_2 + 2 * (total_pairs - (n - 1) - pairs_dist_2)
    print(result)
```

Wait, let me verify with examples:

**Example 1**: n=4, star graph
- Degree of center (1): 3, so pairs at dist 2 = 3*2/2 = 3
- Other nodes have degree 1, so 0
- pairs_dist_2 = 3
- total_pairs = 4*3/2 = 6
- result = (4-1) + 3 + 2*(6 - 3 - 3) = 3 + 3 + 2*0 = 6 ✓

**Example 2**: path 1-2-3-4
- Degrees: node 1:1, 2:2, 3:2, 4:1
- pairs_dist_2 = 0 + 2*1/2 + 2*1/2 + 0 = 0 + 1 + 1 + 0 = 2
- total_pairs = 6
- result = 3 + 2 + 2*(6 - 3 - 2) = 5 + 2*1 = 7 ✓

Perfect!

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a tree with n nodes (subway stations)
2. We add new edges between any two nodes that share a common neighbor in the original tree
3. We need to find the sum of all pairwise distances in the new graph

## Key Insights

1. In the original tree, we have n-1 edges and it's connected
2. When we add edges between any two nodes that share a common neighbor, we're essentially creating a graph where all nodes that are within distance 2 in the original tree become directly connected
3. After adding these edges, what does the new graph look like?

Let me think about this more carefully:

- Two nodes u and v share a common neighbor w if there are edges (u,w) and (v,w) in the original tree
- This means u and v will have a direct connection in the new graph

## Analysis of the Resulting Graph

After adding all such edges:
- Any two nodes at distance 1 (adjacent) already have a direct connection
- Any two nodes at distance 2 (through a common neighbor) will now have a direct connection
- Nodes at distance > 2 will still have distance ≥ 2

But wait - if we add all edges between nodes that share a common neighbor, what happens?

Actually, let's reconsider: if nodes u and v share a common neighbor w, we add edge (u,v).

This means:
- The new graph will have edges for all pairs of nodes at distance ≤ 2 in the original tree
- Any pair of nodes at distance > 2 will still be at distance ≥ 2 (since the direct path is longer)

But actually, if we add edges for all pairs that share a common neighbor, we might be creating a complete graph for each "star" structure.

Let me think about the examples:

**Example 1**: Star graph with center 1 connected to 2,3,4
- Original: distances are 1 for all pairs (since all are connected to center)
- After adding: all pairs have direct connections
- Sum of distances = 1+1+1+1+1+1 = 6 ✓

**Example 2**: Path 1-2-3-4
- Original: distances are (1,2)=1, (1,3)=2, (1,4)=3, (2,3)=1, (2,4)=2, (3,4)=1
- After adding: pairs that share common neighbors
  - 1 and 3: share neighbor 2 → add edge
  - 1 and 4: don't share common neighbor (no w such that (1,w) and (4,w))
  - 2 and 4: share neighbor 3 → add edge
- So edges added: (1,3), (2,4)
- New distances:
  - (1,2)=1, (1,3)=1, (1,4)=2 (path 1-2-4 or 1-3-4)
  - (2,3)=1, (2,4)=1, (3,4)=1
- Sum = 1+1+2+1+1+1 = 7 ✓

## General Solution Strategy

The key insight is that after adding edges between nodes sharing a common neighbor:
- All pairs that are at distance ≤ 2 in the original tree now have distance 1
- All pairs that are at distance > 2 still have distance ≥ 2

So we need to:
1. Calculate the original pairwise distances
2. For each pair, determine if they share a common neighbor (distance ≤ 2)
3. The sum of distances in new graph = sum of distances in original graph with modifications

Actually, let me simplify: in the new graph:
- If two nodes are at distance ≤ 2 in original graph, their distance becomes 1
- If two nodes are at distance > 2 in original graph, their distance remains the same

Wait, that's not quite right either. Even if they're at distance 3 in original, they might still have a path of length 2 through some common structure.

After more thought: when we add edges for all pairs that share a common neighbor, we're creating direct connections for pairs that were previously at distance 2.

So:
- All pairs at distance 1: distance = 1 (unchanged)
- All pairs at distance 2: distance = 1 (after adding edge)
- All pairs at distance > 2: distance ≥ 2 (but what is it?)

For pairs at distance > 2, we need to check if they share some common neighbor. In a tree, two nodes at distance > 2 don't necessarily share a common neighbor.

Actually, two nodes at distance d > 2 in a tree don't share a common neighbor in general. So their distance in the new graph might still be greater than 1.

But let me reconsider: after adding edges for all pairs that share a common neighbor, every pair of nodes that are at most distance 2 apart will have distance 1.

For pairs at distance > 2, the distance in the new graph is at least 2, and might be exactly 2 if they can reach through a path of length 2.

Actually, in a tree, if two nodes are at distance d > 2, they don't share a common neighbor, so no direct edge is added between them. However, they might be able to reach through the new edges.

But in a tree, after adding edges for all pairs that share a common neighbor, the graph becomes such that any two nodes within distance 2 are directly connected.

For nodes at distance > 2, the shortest path might go through some intermediate nodes.

However, looking at the examples and thinking about it, I believe the final answer is:

Sum of distances = (number of pairs at distance 1) × 1 + (number of pairs at distance 2) × 1 + (number of pairs at distance > 2) × (distance in original)

But that's not right either.

Let me go back to the examples and pattern:

**Example 1**: Star graph - all pairs have distance 1 after addition → sum = 6 = C(4,2) × 1

**Example 2**: Path 1-2-3-4
- Pairs with distance 1: (1,2), (2,3), (3,4) → 3 pairs
- Pairs with distance 2: (1,3), (2,4) → 2 pairs (these get edges added)
- Pair with distance 3: (1,4) → stays at distance 2 in new graph? But in the answer it's distance 2

Wait, in example 2, the answer is 7, and the calculation is:
- (1,2)=1, (1,3)=1, (1,4)=2, (2,3)=1, (2,4)=1, (3,4)=1 → sum = 7

So pairs at distance 3 in original become distance 2 in new graph.

The pattern seems to be: after adding edges for pairs that share a common neighbor, the distance between any two nodes that were at distance > 2 becomes 2.

Is this always true?

In a tree, for any two nodes at distance d > 2, they don't share a common neighbor (in the original tree), so no direct edge is added.

But could there be an indirect path of length 2? 

Actually, if two nodes are at distance > 2, they can reach each other with a path of length 2 only if there's a common neighbor, which doesn't exist.

But wait - after adding edges, new edges reduce distances. 

The key insight is that after adding all edges between nodes that share a common neighbor, for any two nodes u,v:
- If d(u,v) ≤ 2 in original tree, then d(u,v) = 1 in new graph
- If d(u,v) > 2 in original tree, then d(u,v) = 2 in new graph

Why? Because the new edges create shortcuts, and any path of length > 2 can often be shortened to length 2.

Actually, let me verify this with a simple case. In a path of 4 nodes: 1-2-3-4.
- Distance between 1 and 4 is 3 in original.
- In new graph: 1-2-3-4 (distance 3), 1-3 (direct), 2-4 (direct), so 1-4 can go through 1-3-4 or 1-2-4, both length 2.
- So yes, distance becomes 2.

For larger distances? In a path 1-2-3-4-5 (distance 4 between 1 and 5):
- In original: distance 4
- After adding: 1-3, 2-4, 3-5 (by common neighbors)
- So 1 can go to 3 (distance 1), 3 to 5 (distance 1), so 1 to 5 is distance 2
- So distance becomes 2

This seems to work! In fact, in any tree after adding edges between nodes that share a common neighbor, the distance between any two nodes becomes at most 2.

Therefore, the sum of distances in the new graph is:
- For every pair, distance = 1 if they were at distance ≤ 2 in original
- For every pair, distance = 2 if they were at distance > 2 in original

But wait, is this always true? Let me think about a different tree.

Actually, I think it's true that after adding these edges, all pairs at distance ≤ 2 become distance 1, and all pairs at distance > 2 become distance 2. This is because the new edges create a "near-complete" graph locally.

So the algorithm:
1. Read the tree
2. Calculate the distance between all pairs in the original tree
3. For each pair, if distance ≤ 2, new distance = 1; if distance > 2, new distance = 2
4. Sum all new distances

But this would be O(n^2) which is too slow for n = 200,000.

We need a more efficient approach.

## Efficient Solution

Let me think about the structure:

In the new graph, every pair of nodes has distance at most 2. The only pairs with distance 2 are those that weren't directly connected and didn't share a common neighbor.

Actually, the pairs with distance > 2 in original tree will have distance 2 in new graph.

So the sum = (number of pairs with distance ≤ 2 in original) × 1 + (number of pairs with distance > 2 in original) × 2

This equals: C(n,2) + (number of pairs with distance > 2 in original)

Because:
- Total pairs = C(n,2)
- Each pair with distance ≤ 2 contributes 1, so contributes 1 to sum
- Each pair with distance > 2 contributes 2, which is 1 + 1, so contributes 1 extra compared to distance 1 case
- Total sum = (C(n,2) × 1) + (number of pairs with distance > 2)

Thus, sum = C(n,2) + (number of pairs with distance > 2 in original tree)

Now I just need to count the number of pairs that are at distance > 2 in the original tree.

How to count this efficiently?

I can use tree DP. For each node, I can compute the distance distribution in subtrees.

But an easier way: total pairs = C(n,2)
Pairs with distance ≤ 2 = pairs at distance 1 + pairs at distance 2

I can count:
- Distance 1 pairs: just the edges in the tree → n-1 pairs
- Distance 2 pairs: pairs of nodes that are connected through a common neighbor

For distance 2 pairs: for each node w, if w has degree d, then it contributes C(d,2) pairs of distance 2.

So total pairs with distance ≤ 2 = (n-1) + Σ(C(degree(w),2) for all w)

Therefore, pairs with distance > 2 = C(n,2) - (n-1) - Σ(C(degree(w),2))

Final sum = C(n,2) + [C(n,2) - (n-1) - Σ(C(degree(w),2))] 
= 2*C(n,2) - (n-1) - Σ(C(degree(w),2))

But wait: let me check with examples.

**Example 1**: n=4, star with center 1 connected to 2,3,4
- C(4,2) = 6
- Distance 1 pairs: 3 (edges 1-2,1-3,1-4)
- Distance 2 pairs: center has degree 3, C(3,2)=3; other nodes degree 1, C(1,2)=0. Total = 3
- So pairs with distance ≤ 2: 3+3=6 = C(4,2)
- Pairs with distance > 2: 0
- Sum = C(4,2) + 0 = 6 ✓

**Example 2**: n=4, path 1-2-3-4
- C(4,2) = 6
- Distance 1 pairs: 3 (edges 1-2, 2-3, 3-4)
- Distance 2 pairs: 
  - Node 1: degree 1 → C(1,2)=0
  - Node 2: degree 2 → C(2,2)=1 (pairs 1,3)
  - Node 3: degree 2 → C(2,2)=1 (pairs 2,4)
  - Node 4: degree 1 → C(1,2)=0
  - Total = 2
- Pairs with distance ≤ 2: 3+2=5
- Pairs with distance > 2: 6-5=1 (pair (1,4))
- Sum = 6 + 1 = 7 ✓

Perfect!

## Final Algorithm

1. Read n and the tree edges
2. Count degree of each node
3. Calculate total_pairs = n*(n-1)//2
4. Calculate distance_1_pairs = n-1
5. Calculate distance_2_pairs = sum of C(degree[i], 2) for all i
6. distance_gt_2_pairs = total_pairs - distance_1_pairs - distance_2_pairs
7. answer = total_pairs + distance_gt_2_pairs
8. Print answer

```python
n = int(input())
if n == 1:
    print(0)
else:
    degree = [0] * (n + 1)
    
    for _ in range(n - 1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        degree[u] += 1
        degree[v] += 1
    
    total_pairs = n * (n - 1) // 2
    distance_1_pairs = n - 1
    distance_2_pairs = 0
    
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        d = degree[i]
        if d >= 2:
            distance_2_pairs += d * (d - 1) // 2
    
    distance_gt_2_pairs = total_pairs - distance_1_pairs - distance_2_pairs
    answer = total_pairs + distance_gt_2_pairs
    
    print(answer)
```

n = int(input())
if n == 1:
    print(0)
else:
    degree = [0] * (n + 1)
    
    for _ in range(n - 1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        degree[u] += 1
        degree[v] += 1
    
    total_pairs = n * (n - 1) // 2
    distance_1_pairs = n - 1
    distance_2_pairs = 0
    
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        d = degree[i]
        if d >= 2:
            distance_2_pairs += d * (d - 1) // 2
    
    distance_gt_2_pairs = total_pairs - distance_1_pairs - distance_2_pairs
    answer = total_pairs + distance_gt_2_pairs
    
    print(answer)

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Sergey Semyonovich is a mayor of a county city N and he used to spend his days and nights in thoughts of further improvements of Nkers' lives. Unfortunately for him, anything and everything has been done already, and there are no more possible improvements he can think of during the day (he now prefers to sleep at night). However, his assistants have found a solution and they now draw an imaginary city on a paper sheet and suggest the mayor can propose its improvements.

Right now he has a map of some imaginary city with $n$ subway stations. Some stations are directly connected with tunnels in such a way that the whole map is a tree (assistants were short on time and enthusiasm). It means that there exists exactly one simple path between each pair of station. We call a path simple if it uses each tunnel no more than once.

One of Sergey Semyonovich's favorite quality objectives is the sum of all pairwise distances between every pair of stations. The distance between two stations is the minimum possible number of tunnels on a path between them.

Sergey Semyonovich decided to add new tunnels to the subway map. In particular, he connected any two stations $u$ and $v$ that were not connected with a direct tunnel but share a common neighbor, i.e. there exists such a station $w$ that the original map has a tunnel between $u$ and $w$ and a tunnel between $w$ and $v$. You are given a task to compute the sum of pairwise distances between all pairs of stations in the new map.


-----Input-----

The first line of the input contains a single integer $n$ ($2 \leq n \leq 200\,000$) — the number of subway stations in the imaginary city drawn by mayor's assistants. Each of the following $n - 1$ lines contains two integers $u_i$ and $v_i$ ($1 \leq u_i, v_i \leq n$, $u_i \ne v_i$), meaning the station with these indices are connected with a direct tunnel.

It is guaranteed that these $n$ stations and $n - 1$ tunnels form a tree.


-----Output-----

Print one integer that is equal to the sum of distances between all pairs of stations after Sergey Semyonovich draws new tunnels between all pairs of stations that share a common neighbor in the original map.


-----Examples-----
Input
4
1 2
1 3
1 4

Output
6

Input
4
1 2
2 3
3 4

Output
7

In the first sample, in the new map all pairs of stations share a direct connection, so the sum of distances is $6$.

In the second sample, the new map has a direct tunnel between all pairs of stations except for the pair $(1, 4)$. ## Problem Understanding

1. We have a tree with n nodes (subway stations)
2. We add new edges between any two nodes that share a common neighbor in the original tree
3. We need to find the sum of all pairwise distances in the resulting graph

## Key Insights

1. In the original tree, two nodes share a common neighbor if they are both adjacent to the same node
2. After adding edges between all pairs that share a common neighbor, we get a new graph
3. For nodes that share a common neighbor, we add a direct edge between them
4. The resulting graph has some special properties

## Analysis of Examples

**Example 1:** Tree with edges (1-2), (1-3), (1-4)
- All nodes (2,3,4) share neighbor 1
- So we add edges (2-3), (2-4), (3-4)
- Now it's a complete graph K4
- Sum of distances = 6 (each pair has distance 1, and there are 6 pairs)

**Example 2:** Tree with edges (1-2), (2-3), (3-4)
- Nodes 1 and 4 don't share a common neighbor
- Nodes 1 and 3 share neighbor 2
- Nodes 1 and 2 share neighbor 2? No, 1 and 2 have common neighbor? 1 only has neighbor 2, 2 has neighbors 1,3
- Nodes 2 and 4 share neighbor 3
- So we add edges: (1-3), (2-4)
- The graph now has edges: (1-2), (2-3), (3-4), (1-3), (2-4)
- Distance between 1 and 4: path 1-2-4 or 1-3-4, both length 2
- Sum of distances = 1+1+1+1+1+2 = 7

The important insight is that for any node w, all its neighbors form a complete subgraph after the edge addition.

So in the final graph:
- For each node w, all neighbors of w are connected to each other (they form a clique)
- The distance between any two nodes that are adjacent to the same node w is 1
- The distance between two nodes that don't share a common neighbor might be longer

## Better Approach

Let me think about the final graph structure:
- For each node w, all its neighbors form a clique
- Two nodes at distance 2 in the original tree (through w) will have distance 1 in the new graph if they share a common neighbor
- Actually, if u and v have a common neighbor w, they get direct connection

Two nodes don't share a common neighbor if they are in different subtrees rooted at different nodes.

Actually, let me implement a solution:

## Solution Structure

1. Build the tree and adjacency list
2. For each node, find all its neighbors (siblings)
3. In the resulting graph, nodes that are siblings (share a common parent) become connected
4. The distance between any two nodes in the same component is 1 if they share a common neighbor
5. Two nodes don't share a common neighbor if they are in different subtrees and not connected through any common neighbor

Wait, I think I'm overcomplicating. Let me implement a simpler approach:

Actually, after adding edges between nodes that share a common neighbor, in the resulting graph:
- Nodes that are in the same "branch" (share a common parent) will be connected to each other
- The distance between two nodes will be at most 2

For any two nodes u and v:
- If they share a common neighbor, distance = 1
- Otherwise, distance = 2 (in a tree, two nodes with no common neighbor must be in different branches)

In a tree, two nodes have no common neighbor if and only if their paths to the root don't share a common intermediate node in a way that creates a common neighbor.

Actually, let me think about the structure more carefully:

After adding edges, the graph becomes such that:
- For each node w, all neighbors of w are connected
- So within each "star" formed by w and its neighbors, all pairs have distance 1

Two nodes that don't share a common neighbor will have distance 2 in the final graph.

So the total sum = (number of pairs with distance 1) + (number of pairs with distance 2)

The pairs with distance 1 are exactly pairs that share a common neighbor in the original tree.

The pairs with distance 2 are pairs that don't share a common neighbor in the original tree.

Let me calculate this:

```python
n = int(input())
if n == 1:
    print(0)
else:
    # Build adjacency list
    adj = [[] for _ in range(n+1)]
    edges = []
    
    for _ in range(n-1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        adj[u].append(v)
        adj[v].append(u)
        edges.append((u, v))
    
    # Count pairs that share a common neighbor
    common_neighbor_pairs = 0
    
    # For each node, count pairs among its neighbors
    for i in range(1, n+1):
        neighbors = adj[i]
        k = len(neighbors)
        if k >= 2:
            common_neighbor_pairs += k * (k - 1) // 2
    
    # Total pairs = n * (n-1) // 2
    total_pairs = n * (n - 1) // 2
    
    # Pairs with distance 1 = pairs that share common neighbor
    # Pairs with distance 2 = total pairs - pairs with distance 1
    
    pairs_with_distance_1 = common_neighbor_pairs
    pairs_with_distance_2 = total_pairs - pairs_with_distance_1
    
    # Total sum of distances
    total_sum = pairs_with_distance_1 * 1 + pairs_with_distance_2 * 2
    
    print(total_sum)
```

- (1,2): distance 1 (direct)

- (3,4): distance 1 (new)

Therefore, every pair has distance 1 after the edge additions.

The key insight is that in the final graph:
- If two nodes have a common neighbor, they have distance 1
- If two nodes do not have a common neighbor, they might still have distance 1 if there's a direct edge or another path

But in the examples, it seems that if two nodes don't have a common neighbor, the distance is 2.

In the path example, (1,4) has no common neighbor and distance is 2.

In the star example, every pair either has a direct edge or shares a common neighbor. The pair (1,2) has a direct edge, so distance 1.

When do two nodes have a direct edge in the original tree? They are adjacent.

When do they have a common neighbor? As above.

For any pair of nodes, if they are not adjacent and don't share a common neighbor, their distance becomes 2.

Otherwise, distance becomes 1.

So the sum of distances is:
- For each pair (u,v): if u and v have a common neighbor OR u and v are adjacent, then distance is 1
- Otherwise, distance is 2

But wait, if they are adjacent, distance is 1 regardless.

So condition for distance 1: either adjacent in original tree or share a common neighbor in original tree.

Otherwise, distance = 2.

In star example:
- All pairs are either adjacent or share a common neighbor → all distance 1 → sum = 6

In path example:
- (1,2): adjacent → distance 1
- (1,3): share common neighbor 2 → distance 1  
- (1,4): not adjacent, no common neighbor → distance 2
- (2,3): adjacent → distance 1
- (2,4): share common neighbor 3 → distance 1
- (3,4): adjacent → distance 1

Sum = 5*1 + 1*2 = 7 ✓

Perfect!

So algorithm:
1. Build adjacency list
2. For each pair (u,v) with u < v:
   - If u and v are adjacent in original tree OR they share a common neighbor, distance = 1
   - Otherwise, distance = 2
3. Sum all distances

But checking all pairs is O(n²) which is too slow for n=200,000.

We need to optimize.

Let me count:
- Total pairs = n*(n-1)//2
- Pairs with distance 2 = pairs that are not adjacent and do not share a common neighbor
- Pairs with distance 1 = total pairs - pairs with distance 2

So sum = 1 * (pairs with distance 1) + 2 * (pairs with distance 2)
= (total pairs - pairs with distance 2) + 2 * (pairs with distance 2)
= total pairs + pairs with distance 2

So sum = total_pairs + (# of pairs that are not adjacent and do not share a common neighbor)

Now, pairs that are not adjacent and do not share a common neighbor:
- These are pairs that are not directly connected and have no common neighbor.

We can count:
- Total pairs: T = n*(n-1)//2
- Pairs that are adjacent: A = n-1
- Pairs that share a common neighbor: C = sum over w of C(degree(w), 2)
- Pairs that are not adjacent and do not share a common neighbor: D = T - A - C

Then sum of distances = T + D = T + (T - A - C) = 2T - A - C

Let me verify:
- Path example: n=4, T=6, A=3 (edges), C = ?
  - w=1: degree 1 → C(1,2)=0
  - w=2: degree 2 → C(2,2)=1
  - w=3: degree 2 → C(2,2)=1  
  - w=4: degree 1 → 0
  - C = 0+1+1+0 = 2
  - D = 6-3-2 = 1
  - Sum = 6 + 1 = 7 ✓

- Star example: n=4, T=6, A=3, C = ?
  - w=1: degree 3 → C(3,2)=3
  - w=2: degree 1 → 0
  - w=3: degree 1 → 0
  - w=4: degree 1 → 0
  - C = 3
  - D = 6-3-3 = 0
  - Sum = 6 + 0 = 6 ✓

Perfect!

So the formula is: sum = 2T - A - C, where:
- T = n*(n-1)//2
- A = n-1 (number of edges in tree)
- C = sum over all nodes w of C(degree(w), 2)

```python
n = int(input())
if n == 1:
    print(0)
else:
    # Build adjacency list
    adj = [[] for _ in range(n+1)]
    
    for _ in range(n-1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        adj[u].append(v)
        adj[v].append(u)
    
    # Total pairs
    total_pairs = n * (n - 1) // 2
    
    # Number of edges (A)
    num_edges = n - 1
    
    # Count pairs that share a common neighbor (C)
    common_neighbor_pairs = 0
    for i in range(1, n+1):
        degree = len(adj[i])
        if degree >= 2:
            common_neighbor_pairs += degree * (degree - 1) // 2
    
    # Sum of distances in new graph
    # = total_pairs + (pairs that are not adjacent and do not share common neighbor)
    # = total_pairs + (total_pairs - num_edges - common_neighbor_pairs)
    # = 2 * total_pairs - num_edges - common_neighbor_pairs
    result = 2 * total_pairs - num_edges - common_neighbor_pairs
    
    print(result)
```
